476. THOMAS LAMBERT was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 1 clock, value 50s., the goods of James Robertson.
JAMES ROBERTSON. I keep a public-house in Stanhope-street, Clare Market - the prisoner and another came in on the 12th of February, and called for a pot of hot - they went into the parlour, where the clock was - I had the paper-hangers at work there the night before, and asked them to go into the tap-room, but they objected - the man with him came out and read the newspaper to me at the bar - this raised my suspicion - I told my servant to keep watch - the man came into the tap-room conversing with me - the servant came and told me the clock was taken down - I went in, and it was lying on the bench and a bag covered over it - I asked him how the dial came there; he said, "I know nothing of it" - he was the only person in the room - I directly sent for a constable, and gave him in charge - I had seen the other man once or twice before.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. The dial was taken down, was it? A. Yes, it was; the whole clock was taken down - it was wrenched from the wall - the other man ran away.
ELIZABETH BUSKIN. I live at the house - on the morning of the 12th of February, the prisoner and a companion came in - his companion came out two or three times, and kept my master in conversation - master told me his suspicions - I went into the parlour, and saw the prisoner coming from a chair or table with a clock in his hand - he dropped it on the seat the moment I entered.
Cross-examined. Q. It was on the settle? A. Yes; I saw him drop it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
( John Chapman, orange and nut merchant, 84, Long-lane, and Joseph Bennett, cabinet-maker, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-162]
477. GEORGE ASDELL was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 1 watch, value 7s., the goods of John Southwell.
JOHN SOUTHWELL. I am a farrier, and live in Tottenham mews - the prisoner used to bring horses to be shod at master's shop - I hung my watch up, on the 10th of February, close to the fire-place in the shop, and missed it- he had been there that morning, and I left him in the shop while I went for master - he pretended to come to warm himself by the fire, as he was out of work - when I returned, he was gone and the watch also - I met him two days afterwards in the mews; I asked him where my watch was - he said he had pawned it - I asked him for the ticket - he said he had not got it, and I gave him in charge - I never suffered him to take it - it was on Monday.
CHARLES WILLIAM TAYLOR. I am a coach-smith - on Monday morning the prisoner came and asked me to buy the watch - I refused - he asked me to pawn it for him - I pawned it at Mr. Crouch's, Grafton-street, for 3s., and gave him the money - he asked me to buy the ticket of him, which I refused.
ROBERT GILES. I am a pawnbroker - I have the watch which was pawned by Taylor for 3s. - it is worth about 8s., but not more. - (Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Confined for Six Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-163]
Second London Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
478. JULIA M'DONALD was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, 1 necklace, value 10s.; 1 shawl, value 8s.; 2 bed-gowns, value 3s.; 1 frock, value 2s.; and 1 napkin, value 1s., the goods of Isaac Isaacs, her master.
SARAH ISAACS. I am the wife of Isaac Isaacs - the prisoner entered my service on the 29th of January - I missed, on the 2d of February, a shawl from the shop - we deal in women's apparel - I afterwards missed some coral beads - when I missed the shawl, I told the prisoner of it, and said, I did not suspect her - she said, when a servant left, I ought to make it a rule to look into her box - that was the night the previous servant went away - I asked her if she knew anything of the coral beads - she said, No, she did not - she said it was very unpleasant that it should be lost - I sent for West, and she was taken into custody - her box was opened by a key, which he took out of her pocket - I suppose she was not present - two night-gowns were found into her box - she had brought that box with her.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Why was she not present when her box was opened? A. The officer took her into custody - my husband has no other name.
SAMUEL HAYES. I am a pawnbroker - I produce a shawl which was pawned at my shop - I do not know who by - the prisoner and her sister are both in the habit of pawning with me.
GEORGE JOHN WEST (City policeman No. 53). I was sent for to take the prisoner into custody - I used no threat to her- she said to her master in my presence, in the way to the Compter, that she hoped he would be as lenient as he possibly could towards her, and she would tell where the beads were; and, as she went up the steps of the Compter, she said they were in the bed between the ticking and flock - I found them there - she said the bed was in the room where the children slept, where her boxes were - I got a key from her at the station-house, with which I opened her box, and found these things in it, which I produce.
SARAH ISAACS re-examined. These things are mine - the bed-gowns are mine, and this frock - this frock was in the tick with the beads, in a napkin - I can only speak to the frock and beads, by my mark.
Prisoner. Master asked where the beads were - I told him - he said he would forgive me if I told - I said I know nothing of anything else.
GEORGE JOHN WEST re-examined. He said, "If you will tell me what you have done with the beads and the shawl, I should be very sorry to hurt you; if you will tell where they are, I will forgive you" - she said she would tell where the beads were, but she knew nothing about the shawl.
SARAH ISAACS re-examined. I know the frock - I have a gown of the same, and I know my own work on it - it is a baby's frock - the napkin and gown are mine.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Recommended to mercy. Confined for Three Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-164]
479. GEORGE THOMPSON was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PUNTER. I am a cow-keeper and carman, and live in Northumberland-alley, Fenchurch-street - the prisoner has been in my service ever since last November twelvemonth, as clerk - it was his duty to take any money which was brought to the yard to be paid for milk - there was a book in which he was to enter the money received - that was the only account I could have from him of money received - on the 16th January I said it was very wrong to take my money in this way, and how could I pay his wages when he took my money - he said, "I am only saving it to give to you all in a lump" - I said, "You had no business to do that; I shall give you in charge of an officer" - he said, "Do not give yourself any trouble, I will go and fetch an officer" - he went out of the gate, and I did not see him again till I took him into custody, which was about ten days afterwards- he had called in the mean time, when I was not there, and left his address - I have his book here - there is no entry of 2s. 8d., received from Charles Fellowes on account of Atkinson, on the 6th of January; nor of 15s., from Tillman, on the 8th of January; nor of 2s. 9d., from Mrs. Jewson on the 13th of January - they were all customers of mine, and had accounts running for milk.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. The accounts were made up weekly, were they not? A. No; every night the money taken in the day-time was given to me - this is the daily account - the milk business is only a trifling concern, and I settled that account every night, and the carman's business was settled every morning - there was an account settled on the 6th of January, between me and the prisoner - that was not a weekly account, but a day's account - nothing was said about settling accounts on Tuesday, the 14th - he had accounted to me every night until the 14th, according to the book - there was no weekly account independent of the nightly account, settled up to the 14th - there was no weekly account at all.
Q. Do you remember, on Wednesday, the 15th of January, the prisoner asking you for the book the accounts were made up in? A. Yes; I had left it at home, and given him another book instead - I have that here - the book he asked for was the book I have produced - and here is the one I gave him, when he asked for the other - both of them are in his handwriting - the book was given up to me every evening, and I took it home to enter the settlement - I had left it at home, and bought him a new one to go on with, and gave it him on the 15th - he never said hewould pay me the balance - we had no quarrel as to the mode the entries were made - he did not offer that anybody should make up the books, and say he would pay me - I did not take the key out of his desk - I was not at home when he called on the 17th.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. I see, by the book, the sums received are in small amounts of 2s. and 3s. each? A. Yes, here is what was paid for, and what was on credit - I never heard of any claim from him until I was before the Lord Mayor.
CHARLOTTE FELLOWES. I am servant to Mr. Atkinson, a cheesemonger, in Fenchurch-street - on the 6th of January, I paid the prisoner 2s. 8d. on his master's account, at the counting house - he gave me a receipt with his own hand - but we never keep those receipts.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever paid him any money before? A. Several times.
HENRY TILLMAN. My father takes milk from Mr. Punter - on the 8th of January, I paid the prisoner 15s., on his master's account - he wrote a receipt on the bill; this is it (looking at it) - I saw him write it - I paid him the money at his master's house.
FRANCES JEWSON. I am the daughter of Mr. Jewson- I paid the prisoner 2s. 9d., on his master's account, at the counting-house, on the 13th of January - I took a receipt for the bill - it being so small a sum, my mother burnt it.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been fifteen months in his employ, and every day I had property of his in East India Company warrants, to the amount of some thousand pounds- every thing about the premises was left entirely under my charge - if you look at the books, you will find I rendered him an account up to the 6th of January, of all bills owing from his customers, every day - I ought to have made out another account on the Wednesday following - it was a weekly account, and during that week I had received this money - he came to town on Wednesday - I said, "Sir, where is the book, that I may make up my accounts for the week?" - he said, "I shall not give it to you"- I said, "Why, how can I make up the accounts?" - he said, "Make them up as you please" - and about twelve o'clock he brought me the new book - on Thursday, I asked him again for the book - he said, "Go on with the new one, you shall not have the old one again" - I said, "I cannot tell what the people owe without the old book" - on Thursday night, he said, "What have you received from the milk people this week?" - I said, "15s. of Gilliman, but the account was 19s. 3d. - I have also received 2s. 9d. from Atkinson, and 2s. 8d. from Jewson" - I said, "As I am about leaving you on Friday, and there is some little dispute between us, if you will appoint any third person tomorrow (there was a trifle of difference between us about coach hire), I shall be glad to meet your friend, and settle every thing" - he said, "I will see you d - d first," and walked out - after he was gone home at night, I missed my key out of my desk, and could never hear about it - I went next morning, about a quarter to eight o'clock, and found his nephew in possession of my desk, which was open - he said his uncle had ordered I should not have the key, nor the books, any more, and he must do as he told him - I said, "Well, give me a piece of paper" - I wrote my direction on it and said, "When your uncle comes to town, I will settle everything with him, whenever he likes" - I have subpoened his nephew here.
JOHN HILL. I am the prosecutor's nephew - I am employed in my uncle's business - it was usual for the prisoner to make up the accounts every week for the milk business, either on Monday or Tuesday - to the best of my knowledge that account was made up weekly - I remember the prisoner coming to me on the 17th - I was at the desk at which he was usually seated - he said, "John, I know your orders, will you deliver the key to me?" - I said, "I will not" - he immediately took a piece of paper, and wrote his direction down, "George Thompson, No. 1, Portland-street, Stepney," and said, "Tell your uncle, if he wants me, that is where I am to be found" - I did not go to look for him there - I believe he has stated to me, that he was ready to settle his accounts with my uncle, but never to my uncle.
COURT. Q. Did he offer to deliver his accounts, saying he was ready? A. Not to me, nor in my presence.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did not he ask for the book? A. I never heard him ask for the book.
COURT. Q. Do you know to what amount he received money for Mr. Punter, every week? A. I do not - I have seen him settling with the men.
Mr. ADOLPHUS. Q. Is this the book in which he kept his accounts? A. Yes; these are daily accounts - when the man comes back, from taking out the milk, he settles with him - there are two columns, one for paid and the other for unpaid - Mr. Punter took it home to Clapham with him every night.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-165]
480. SAMUEL KING was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of December, 48 coffin handles, value 1s. 6d.; 96 coffin handle plates, value 16s.; 70 coffin plates, value 12s.; and 24 loops, value 9d.; of John Gardner and others, his masters.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH EDWARDS. I live in Little Alie-street, and am a maker of coffin plates, and such things - I was in the prosecutor's employ for nearly thirteen years - I left him last July - a man named Samuel Lazarus brought some things to me to sell the day before new year's day - I was at the Bell public-house, opposite Whitechapel church - I was still employed in the same trade - I had seen Lazarus before - while I was employed at the prosecutor's, I acquired a knowledge of their manufacture - he employed me to sell coffin handles, and a variety of ornaments for coffins - I could tell them directly to be Mr. Gardner's manufacture - I did not buy them - I detained them, and gave information to Mr. Davis, the prosecutor's clerk, and gave him the articles.
Cross-examined by Mr. ADOLPHUS. Q. How long were you in the prosecutor's employ? A. Nearly thirteen years - I was discharged - I had had a little trouble concerning family affairs - I was rather given up to drink - I have served several undertakers with coffins - have attended funerals, but have not been regularly employed since - I am not now employed by the prosecutor - I do not know where Lazarus is - persons come to the coffin plate shops, andbuy the tin cuttings - I was acquainted with Lazarus by his coming to the shop for this sort of cuttings and things - he lived somewhere in Spitalfields, within two or three hundred yards of the Bell - he came to sell them to me to sell again - I was to pay the money for them - the prisoner was not in the prosecutor's employ when I was - he came afterwards - I have not expressed any determination to get into my old place at all events - nothing like it- it has been said so, but there is no truth in it - I have not been at their warehouse since July.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-166]
481. JAMES SMITH and THOMAS CLARKE were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of January, 6 hats, value 30s., the goods of Richard Brown, from the person of Samuel John Smart .
SAMUEL JOHN SMART. I am servant to Richard Brown - on Thursday, the 9th of January, I was sent by my master with half-a-dozen hats to Mr. Myers, Hounsditch - and in Fenchurch-street I saw Clarke - he began talking to me, asked where I was going, and where I lived, and I told him - he then said he was going down Mark-lane, and turned into Mincing-lane - he came over in about five minutes, and said, "Have not you got farther?" - I said, "No" - he said, "I am going down a court opposite the East India House, and will walk with you" - then Smith came up and said, "Are not you going from Mr. Brown's to Mr. Myers'?"- I said, "Yes" - he said, "I have just come from Mr. Brown's, who said, if I made haste I should overtake you; you are to give me the hats, and to return home" - I said,"I have got to get the bill signed" - he said, "Mr. Brown said that was of no consequence" - I gave him the hats - he asked me to go down a court with him, which I did - and then he said the person was not at home - he was going away, and then said, "I am going down Cornhill, go on, and I will overtake you," and I saw no more of him - I went home and told master - Clarke had got out of me where I was going - and then Smith came up and seemed to know all about it - on the 15th I saw them together in Long-alley, and gave them into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Is your master here? A. Yes - I had never seen Smith before - he was with me a very short time - when he said he had come from Mr. Brown's, I gave up the written direction - I am sure they are the two men - they each had got a brown coat on, and an umbrella - I am sure of Smith by the umbrella and coat, and by his face - I knew him again directly I saw him- I saw him again six days afterwards, and was quite sure of him - master had directed me to leave the hats at Mr. Myers's, and get the book signed - Clarke first came up to me in Fenchurch-street, about five minutes' walk from Cornhill.
RICHARD BROWN. I am a hatter, and live in Pump-court, Union-street, Borough - I gave nobody any directions to overtake Smart and get the hats, nor were the hats to be delivered to anybody but Myers.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you other persons in your establishment who manage your business? A. No - nobody sells hats but myself.
Smith's Defence. I never saw the boy before I was taken into custody - I do not know Clarke at all.
Clarke's Defence. I never saw the boy before in my life - as to the charge, I am as innocent as a child unborn - I believe I have persons to prove I was at work at the time.
S. A. SMART re-examined. I am sure they are the men - it was between three and four o'clock in the afternoon.
( William Hooper, broker and undertaker, 1, Gloucester-buildings, Upper Whitecross-street, and Joseph Walley, plumber and glazier, 12, Hamilton-place, New-road, gave the prisoner Smith a good character; - and John Thomas, cord-wainer, 24, Willow-street, Finsbury, John Morris, Gibraltar-walk, Bethnal-green, and James Baldwin, gave the prisoner Clarke a good character.)
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 22.
CLARKE - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Of Stealing only. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-167]
482. JAMES DRISCOLL was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of January, 1 snuff-box, value £2 10s., the goods of John Cutmore, from his person.
JOHN CUTMORE. I live at No. 107, Bunhill-row - on Sunday evening, the 26th of January, between eight and nine o'clock, I was crossing West Smithfield, with my wife - we got partly across - she stopped suddenly - I turned round and saw the prisoner and another person - I ran after the prisoner, and overtook him in Cloth-fair - I caught him by the collar, and called out to a person whom I knew, who came to my assistance, and took hold of his other collar - I told him I had been robbed of a silver snuff-box, and it was taken out of the prisoner's right hand and given to me - I am sure it was in his possession.
EMMA CUTMORE. I was walking with my husband across Smithfield - I heard a footstep behind me - I suddenly turned round, and saw two men - the prisoner was one of them - the other had one hand at the tail of my husband's coat, and the other hand in his pocket - he immediately took his hand out, and handed the snuff-box to the prisoner, who ran away - I held the other one for about twenty minutes in the street - an old gentleman came behind me and shook me, and compelled me to let go of him - I should know that man again, because, after the man had got away, he stood and talked to me a long time, and said, I ought to have been taken in charge for holding the man; and, if a policeman had been near, he would have given me in charge- I explained it to him, and gave him my address - I waited about ten minutes - my husband did not return - I have not described the old man to the police, for I was not able to go out for a long time afterwards; for the man bit my hand, and trod on my foot - I could not get my shoe on.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. It is my first offence, I beg the mercy of the Court.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-168]
483. CHARLES NEWTON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of February, 1 coat, value 25s., the goods of John Cordy Baxter.
HOWARD HALL. I am in the employ of Mr. John Cordy Baxter, who is a pawnbroker, and lives on Snow-hill - on the 1st of February I was in the shop - I did not see the prisoner at the door - I saw him running up the hill with acoat thrown over his shoulder - I looked and missed a coat from the door before I saw him - Kempsey, the foreman, ran after him, and brought him back with the coat, which is Mr. Baxter's.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. The foreman is not here? A. No - I will swear I saw the coat over the man's shoulder - nobody was with him at the time.
WILLIAM BARROW. I am a labourer, and live at No. 47, Featherstone-street, City-road - about half-past three o'clock on the afternoon of the 1st of February, I was going up Snow-hill - I saw the prisoner and another young man talking together at the corner of a court next to Mr. Baxter's shop - the other young man went down Snow-hill - the prisoner turned into the shop - I saw the elbow of the prisoner raised, as if he was taking the coat - he ran up the hill with the coat, pursued by the foreman, who brought him back, with it in his possession.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was the coat? A. Hanging up inside the door - I could just see his elbow when he lifted his hand up to take it - I believe it was inside the shop - I cannot say whether it was on the left or right side of the door - the prisoner, and no other person, is the man who took the coat - I was about three yards from the shop when he came out with it, and eight paces off when I saw him taking it - I was on the same side of the way - I never said I was on the opposite side - I do not think it was quite half-past three o'clock - I have been a labourer all my life - I worked at the London Docks last Tuesday and Wednesday - I was in the police before that - that was my first employment - I was discharged from the police on the 19th of December, in consequence of a misunderstanding which took place between me and another man - I mentioned the man's name in a public-house, and he was not present; and when I saw him on the Saturday night, I told him what I had said - he said I had spoken of his character when he was not present; and I was discharged, as he reported me - I never charged that man with any offence - I know Mr. Holley - I never charged him with anything - I was not discharged for using any improper language to his wife.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I met with a friend in Holborn who had come from the country - I went into Thompson's, and had something to drink - on coming out, I met a friend - he had taken his place in Smithfield to go to St. Albans - we met a friend - he said, "Are you going over Blackfriars-bridge?" - I said, "No; I am going with my friend to Smithfield" - he said, "I bought a coat; if you will wait two minutes, I will go and fetch it" - he went off, and in a short time came again, and said, "You may as well come" - I said, "I will go with you" - he said, "Carry this for me" - I took the coat, put it on my arm, and was walking away, when the shopman came and said, "That is not your coat" - I said, "Certainly not - there is the person who bought it" - he did not say, I must go to him, but took me back.
JOHN BLIZARD. I am in the employ of Mr. Alderman Birch at times, and have been so ten or eleven years - on Saturday, 1st of February, I was in Holborn, coming from Donovan's the Poulterer's, at near three o'clock - I met the prisoner near Thompson's with another man, coming out - they asked me to take something to drink, and I went back with them to Thompson's - we all came out together, and walked up Snow-hill, towards Smithfield - the other man said, that he was going to St. Albans by the coach - he asked the prisoner to walk so far with him, for he had bought a greatcoat at a pawnbroker's close by - they walked on together about four or five yards - the man who was with the prisoner left us by the pawnbroker's, up the hill near Smithfield - I don't know the pawnbroker's name - we two walked on about five or six yards - I knew the prisoner before, when he kept the Devonshire Arms, Golden-lane - we were joined by the other man - he had a great-coat with him - he gave it to the prisoner, whether it was for inspection or not, I cannot say; for I said I could not stop a moment longer - I was going over the water to my master, who keeps the Surrey-gate Tavern - the coat was a dark brown great-coat- I was at work at Mr. Alderman Birch's on the Lord Mayor's-day as cook, at Merchant Taylors ' Hall, and have done so upwards of ten years - I have known the prisoner about seven years.
COURT. Q. In what direction did they go when you left them? A. Towards Smithfield - the pawnbroker's shop is in the narrow part as you go to Smithfield, on the left hand side - it is up a hill - I do not know whether it is Snow-hill - I was going to the Surrey and Kent Tavern - it was formerly called Blizard's Hotel - this happened five or six yards from the pawnbroker's - I did not see the prisoner after I wished them good day - I do not know the name of the person - he was a tall man, about my own height - I never saw him before or since.
JURY to HOWARD HALL. Q. Was the prisoner running or walking when you first saw him? A. A sort of a trot, running as it were, going fast, not walking - he had nobody with him - the coat was dark brown - I did not run after him at all - I saw him at the door - I missed the coat from the door, and saw him going up the hill with it - he was not out of my sight until he was taken, from the time he took the coat, which was, as near as I can recollect, half-past three o'clock - it was not half a minute from the time I missed it till I saw it on his shoulder - I was not behind the counter when the coat was taken - I was near the door when I missed it - the same person I saw running with the coat was brought back with it by the foreman - I did not lose sight of him a moment - he was taken eight yards from the shop, at most - it was at Mr. Jones's door, the cook-shop, about six yards from master's door - the prisoner struck at the foreman, and began to show fight - when he was taken he said a man gave it him to carry - the foreman darted out of the shop and seized him in a moment with it.
W. BARROW re-examined. I saw the prisoner stopped - there was no man at all near him to give him the coat - the man that was with him went down the hill before the prisoner took the coat - the prisoner turned to the right into the shop - I never saw Blizard before to-night - there was no man there at all except the prisoner when he took the coat - the prisoner was taken within seven yards of the door - the other man walked sharp down the hill - I had my two children, one in each hand, and did not run after him.
COURT to H. HALL. Q. Describe the situation of the shop where you were? A. I almost that moment come downfrom dinner - I came to the door and missed the coat - the prisoner could hardly have got out of the shop when I missed it - the foreman was at the end of the counter near the door, just inside it - the door opens just where you go behind the counter - there is no door to the counter - the foreman stood about a yard and a half from the door - there is no flap to lift up to the counter - there is a flap, but it is always open.
W. BARROW re-examined. I saw the prisoner take the coat - I did not call "Stop thief" - I did not know he was stealing - I saw him pursued - I could not tell but what he was the pawnbroker.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-169]

490. JOSEPH MILLARD was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of January, 6lbs. of copper, value 4s. 6d., the goods of John Seaward and others, his masters; and JOHN HOPSON was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen, against the Statute; to which Millard pleaded
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Recommended to mercy by the prosecutor. - Confined Five Days.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and PARKER conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY SCOBEL. I am a foreman of the engineers to Mr. John Seaward and Co. - on Saturday, the 4th of January, I ordered two quarters and eight pounds of copper bolts to be sent to the smiths' forge were Millard worked, to be cut up for melting, and to be returned to the brass shop - I was engaged in my vice shop, turning - about an hour afterwards I saw Millard near the brass shop, with a piece of copper bolt in his hand, which he appeared to be playing with - I followed him to the edge of the wharf, and saw him endeavouring to put something under his apron - I went to the brass shop and weighed the metal - I missed six pounds of copper bolt - I saw Millard leave the premises at one o'clock to go to dinner - I followed him to Salmon-lane, and went into a new beer-shop, opposite Hopson's premises - I saw Millard pass his premises, and then return back - Hopson has two shops, one is a harness shop, and one an iron shop - Hopson was standing at the door of the harness shop - Millard passed him and went into the iron shop - I afterwards went into Hopson's.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How soon did you go? A. I should think in about five minutes - the policeman went in before me, and afterwards beckoned me over - I saw some copper bolt, which the policeman said Hopson took from the scale - when Hopson was told he must go to the station-house he said, "Let us go out at the back door, at all events."
JAMES CLARK (police-constable). On the 4th of January, I was directed to watch Millard - when he left his master's, he went home, to a street I do not know the name of - he came out in about ten minutes, and went to Hopson's shop - he went in at the iron shop-door - I did not see Hopson then - there is a kitchen at the back of the harness shop, and you go through the iron shop, and turn to the left hand to go to it - I went in in about a minute and a half after Millard; I saw him standing on one side of the table, and Hopson on the other - the cloth was laid for dinner, and, on the cloth, stood a small weighing machine, and this copper was in the scale - as soon as I got to the door, Hopson grabbed the copper off the scale with his right hand - there was a four-pound weight and a small weight in the other scale, and he took the small weight in his left hand, and put both his hands behind him - he went back a few paces, and stood against the wall - there was no other person in the room but the housekeeper - I asked Hopson, two or three times, what he had in his hands - he said, "Nothing" - I pulled his hands forward, and found these three pieces of copper in his right hand, and this two-pound weight in his left - the copper weighed aboutsix pounds - I said he must go with me - he asked if I was an officer, as I was not in uniform - I said I was - he did not say anything to Millard or to me.
Q. Pray recollect yourself - did he say anything to you? A. Yes; he said was there no way of settling it; was there no way of making it up? - I said so to the magistrate - I do not remember his saying anything more.
Q. Did he say anything to you on the road to the station-house? A. In going to Lambeth-street, he said it was in my power to make it as easy as I liked, and he was very sorry I was not a moment later - I asked him, why? - he said he was going to throw it into the street - I went back to his house, after he was in the station, and found this chisel and drill - Millard was taken to the station, and a half-crown, a shilling, and a sixpence, were found on him - there is a board up at Hopson's shop with the prices he gives for different things.
Cross-examined. Q. What is on the board? A."Copper, 6d., brass, 5d." - I think every one can see it - I have stated the same here as I did at the police-office - my words were taken down and read over to me, and I was desired to sign it.
COURT. Q. Are you sure you told the magistrate that he said he was about to throw the copper into the street, that he said, "Is there no way of settling it?" and that he said it was in your power to make it as easy as you liked? A. Yes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you been in the police? A. Four years - there were two examinations, and the depositions were taken on the second examination - on the first time they were taken in a book.
HENRY SCOBEL. These pieces of copper are my master's - these instruments I decline to swear to, but there is"ard" on this chisel, which I think is part of my master's stamp. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-176]

492. JAMES DAY was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 cloak, value 24s., the goods of Edward Clement and another.
GEORGE PHELPS. I am in partnership with Mr. Edward Clement - we are linen-draper s at Poplar - on the 11th of February we had this cloak hanging just withinside the door, at eleven o'clock in the day - it was brought to the door by the witness about five minutes after eleven o'clock.
WILLIAM CLEMENS. I was at my house, and saw the prisoner and three others come down about eleven o'clock- they parted company - the prisoner and one other went toward the prosecutor's shop - they then came and stood near my shop again, and then crossed to the prosecutors - the other one took the cloak and gave it to the prisoner - they again crossed towards my house - I walked out to them and took the prisoner - he threw down the cloak, and I took it up.
Prisoner. I had been out of work a long time, and we took it.

[trialAccount t18340220-178]
493. BENJAMIN BUCKLE was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of February, 7 caps, value 7s., the goods of James Spencer; and WILLIAM CREW was indicted for feloniously receiving the goods aforesaid, well knowing them to have been stolen.
JAMES SPENCER. I keep a sale-shop, Batchelor-row, Battle-bridge - on Friday night, the 7th of February, about half-past nine o'clock, I missed seven caps, which had been inside the shop - I saw them again the same evening in a chair at Crew's, the prisoner's house, in Bagnigge Wells-road, about a quarter of a mile from my house - he is a dealer in marine stores - he has a shop; but I found him in his private house - he said the caps had been brought there to be sold.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was anybody else there when he told you this? A. His wife was, and Buckle might have been.
WILLIAM HOLLINGSWORTH. I live next door to the prosecutor - on the 7th of February, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, I saw Buckle pass my door with something in his hands, which induced me to go and ask Mr. Spencer if he had lost anything; and I told him which way Buckle went; I remained at the shop door, and in a few minutes I saw a boy at the window, who, I thought, was connected with him, and I taxed him with it; and from what he said, he and I went after Mr. Spencer; he took us to Crew's house, in York-buildings - he went and tapped at the shutter twice with two signal knocks - he then knocked at the door, and wished us to wait outside; but I pressed forwards, and we went in; and on a chair by the side of Crew were the caps, which Spencer identified as his, and asked him how they came there - Crew said they were brought there for sale.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not Crew's shop shut up? A. I do not know - he did not say that he had declined to purchase the caps.
THOMAS GRIFFIN. I lodge at Crew's, in Hamilton-row, York-buildings - he keeps a rag and iron shop, and buys all sorts of things - he generally shuts up about eight o'clock, but he buys things in the house after that - on the night of the 7th of February I went with Buckle - we stopped at Mr. Spencer's shop, and Buckle took the caps - he lived at Crew's, the same as I did - we set out a little after eight o'clock - after Buckle took the caps I was waiting till he came back, and the gentleman took me to the prosecutor, - I took them to Crew's, because I knew Buckle would take the caps there - I gave two taps at the shutter for them to let me in, which is the way we all knock, and Buckle opened the door - we lodge, and eat and drink there sometimes, not always - we generally get up at seven or eight o'clock, and go to bed at nine or ten o'clock - when I lived with Crew, I generally used to carry out phials to serve the doctors - I have been out with Buckle in an evening, and stolen things, which we have sold to Crew.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not tell Buckle to take the caps to Crew's? A. Yes; and to tell Crew to allow them to remain there - I did not hear Crew say that he had refused to buy them, that he would have nothing to do with them.
WILLIAM COLSON. I am an officer - I went to Crew's, and asked him about these caps - he said they were brought to his house for sale, but he did not buy them.
WILLIAM REYNOLDS. I am an officer - I assisted in taking the prisoner - these are the caps.
MR. SPENCER. These are mine - I know this one in particular; it has been mine for six months - it has been discoloured by being at the door.
Buckle's Defence. As I and Griffin were going to Somers Town, I saw these caps in the foot-path; I took them up, and he said, "Take them to Mr. Crew's, and ask him to buy them," and he said he did not like to have them at his place.
(W. Ward, a street-keeper of Christ's Hospital; William Corket, a bedstead maker; John Yeoman, a hatter; gave Crew a good character.)
BUCKLE - GUILTY. Aged 11.
Transported for Seven Years.
CREW - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-179]
494. THOMAS FRANCIS was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 bridle-bit, value 15s., the goods of Samuel Snape.
SAMUEL SNAPE. On the 8th of February the prisoner came home to my house - I put the bridle-bit on the table and told my wife to wipe it and put it by while I went to Drury-lane - when I came back it was gone - I have known the prisoner seven or eight years; his father and mother make list shoes.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you give me the bit to take to the corner of Newton-street, and wait till you come? A. No; I did not.
ELIZABETH SNAPE. I am the prosecutor's wife - my husband came home with the prisoner - my husband told me to wipe the bit; I did so, and put it on a chair - the prisoner took it up and said he would meet Snape at the corner of Newton-street with it - he took it away rolled up in paper - I had not heard my husband tell him to take it to Newton-street.
SAMUEL SNAPE. When I heard that he was gone to Newton-street, I went there, but did not see him till Tuesday, when I heard he was taken.
RICHARD CHAPMAN (police-constable F 18). I took the prisoner - he said he knew nothing of the bit.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor gave me the bit to take to the corner of Newton-street and wait till he came; I took it, and a man came to me and said he came from Mr. Rowley's; I said I came from one of his workmen - he took the bit and gave me 6d. - this woman keeps a bad house.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Seven Years.

FRANCIS WILLIAM STEBBINGS. 495. JOHN GROVE was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of February, 30 fathoms of rope, value 30s., the goods of George Turner.
JOSEPH BAUM. I am horse-keeper to Mr. George Turner - on the 3d of February I was at his house, and saw the prisoner pulling some rope towards him, to get it on his back - he had no business to be there - I asked what he wanted with the rope - he said, "To go down with the barge" - I let him go out, thinking it was all right.
CHARLES GOLDEN. I am a carman - I met the prisoner in the stable with some rope on his back - I said nothing to him, nor he to me - we had no barge going down that day - he was taken the same night, quite intoxicated - he had some cash in his pocket.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw a man who told me to go and get a coil of rope - he said Joseph Baum was there, and he would give it to me - I went - he was there with a light - I took the rope to the bargeman, and he gave me a pot of beer.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-182]
496. ELIZA GLASGOW was indicted for stealing, on the 8th February, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of Mary Finley, she having been before convicted of felony.
MARY FINLEY. I keep a haberdasher's shop in Stepney - on the 8th of February, at half-past two o'clock, the prisoner came into my shop and asked to look at some silk handkerchiefs - I showed her one out of the window - she said it was too thin - I then turned some out of a box - a man came in without shoes, begging - I said I had nothing to give him - he snatched the handkerchief, which I was showing to the prisoner, and ran away - I had seen him standing on the left of the window when the prisoner came in - I ran to the door and cried "Stop thief!" but not knowing which way the man run, I returned to the shop, recollecting that I had left the handkerchiefs on the counter - I then pushed the prisoner to the door, but Mrs. Freeman called to me to detain her, as she belonged to the man - I then told her, if she would tell me where the man was, that I might get the handkerchief, she might go - but she would not.
SARAH FREEMAN. I live opposite Mrs. Finley - I was standing at my window about half-past two o'clock, and saw the prisoner come up the street, and stop at the window - a young man was with her without shoes and stockings - they were conversing together, and stopped at her window - they were talking together - the prisoner went into the shop - the young man waited by the window - I saw Mrs. Finley show the prisoner some handkerchiefs, and the man went in and snatched the handkerchief, and came out - I saw it was a red handkerchief - he folded it up and put it in his bosom - I had seen them about three minutes, and looking in at the window.
JOHN JAMES (police-constable, K 256.) I took the prisoner - she had no money on her, nothing but a small knife - I took her about twenty minutes before three o'clock.
JOHN ROBERTSON. I was a constable - I have a certificateof the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office - I know her to be the person (read).
Prisoner's Defence. I was going up the street, a young man came and asked me to go and look at a handkerchief for him - I said, "Yes, if you give me the money"- we went on, there was one in the window - he said, "I want a deeper red one" - I went in and asked for it - he came in and stole it.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-183]
497. THOMAS JENKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1 hat-case, value 30s., the goods of the Honourable George Anson; and 1 hat, value 10s., and 1 glove, value 6d., the goods of John Wilson .
JOHN WILSON. I am footman to the Honourable George Anson, of Curzon-street - I was with his carriage on the 13th of February - it stopped at his door - I got down, and a woman gave me some information - I pursued the prisoner in the way she directed me, and overtook him by Derby-street - I caught hold of him in the middle of the road, and he dropped the hat-case - I had not noticed it since we left the Custom-house - we arrived at Curzon-street about half-past five o'clock - it had been buckled on the carriage at my side - my hat and gloves were in it.
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. What did you do at the Custom-house? A. We had just arrived from Calais - we were at the Custom-house about two hours - we were not detained about any gloves - there was some fog this evening - there were people about - I cannot tell where it was lost - the woman who stated she saw it taken is not here.
JOHN JONES. I am helper in the prosecutor's stables - I lodge at the White Lion, in Derby-street - I was standing at the corner, and the prisoner passed me - Wilson was pursuing him - the prisoner dropped the hat-case - they had a scuffle - the prisoner got away, a young man who was with me picked the case up.
SAMUEL WISE (police-constable A 86). I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw the prisoner run through Stanhope-gate - I took him back - this case and hat were brought to me.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been at Paddington - I had an appointment to meet a young man, and was running to meet him, when I was taken.
WILLIAM CLIFFEN. I am a plasterer, and live in Shouldham-street - I saw the man who dropped the hat-case- it was not the prisoner.
COURT. Q. Where had you been? A. To May-fair; I was coming across Curzon-street, and saw a man running across the road, and another ran to meet him - they met in the road, the tall one struck the other, and the hat-case was dropped, and it rolled towards the gutter - I really think the prisoner is not the tall one, and I do not think the other one was Wilson - they both ran off - the tall one had a long great-coat, but whether it was black, blue, or brown, I cannot tell, as the night was so foggy - I could not see two yards from me - I ran towards Park-lane, to near where the prisoner was caught - I lost sight of both the men - when I first saw the two men in the scuffle, my two sons were with me, and could see the men, but they could not run fast enough to keep up with me - to the best of my belief, neither the prisoner nor Wilson are the men- I saw the prisoner in the custody of Wise, within two or three minutes after I saw him run away - he ran down Audley-street, and then Stanhope-street - a man, who appeared like a groom, picked up the hat-case, he was not like Wilson - I was about twenty yards behind the short man, when he ran after the tall man - when the prisoner was taken the tall man was gone.
CHARLES CLIFFEN. I was with my father, and saw the scuffle about the hat-case, it was a foggy night - I do not think the prisoner is the man who dropped it.
COURT. Q. How old are you? A. Fourteen - I have heard the prisoner's name since then - I never saw him at my father's house - I saw a man run away, he was not so tall as the prisoner - he had a long coat on - Wilson is the man who ran after him - when I first saw him there was a bit of a bustle in the middle of the road - I saw the man drop the hat-case, it did not roll that I saw - Wilson took it up and ran down a mews - my father went on, and we went on after him - I lost sight of the tall man when he turned the corner.
COURT to JOHN WILSON. Q. Are you the person who had scuffle with some person in Curzon-street? A. Yes; he dropped the hat-case, then ran, and I pursued him - I did not lose sight of him for a moment - as he was going to turn round Stanhope-street, there was a family coming out of a house, and he turned across the road, and ran towards Stanhope-gate, and the policeman caught him - the prisoner is the man - there was no other tall man present.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you undertake to say there were no other men in the street? A. Not that I saw, except the servants - I will not say there were not.
COURT to SAML. WISE. Q. When you took the prisoner, was he running? A. Yes, as fast as possible - I heard the cry of "Stop thief" two or three times - the moment I turned the prisoner round, Wilson came up - I saw no other men running, but some footmen who came up afterwards - Cliffen identified the prisoner as the man Wilson had scuffled with.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-184]
498. JOHN KELLY and JOHN MILLS were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February; 3 pewter pots, value 4s., the goods of John Warner, and 1 pewter pot, value 15d., the goods of James Alehouse .
THOMAS GOODYEAR (police-constable, E 39). I saw the prisoners on the 1st of February, in Oxford-market - I followed them down Oxford-street, as far as Hanway-street - I saw Kelly turn round and say, "Piper," - meaning that I was watching them - when he said that, he went across the road - then Mills went down Charles-street, towards Soho-square - I found that they were parting - I ran and seized him - I found on him one pint pot - I then snatched hold of his collar, and run him down Oxford-street, where I overtook Kelly - I put him into a tobacconist's shop - I found three pots on him - Mills has a pocket made on purpose for pot stealing all round him.
JAMES ALEHOUSE. I keep the Edinburgh Castle, in Welbeck-street - this pewter pot is mine; it is worth about 1s. 3d. - it was found on Mills - I have lost five dozen within the last five months.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I am in the service of Mr. Warner - he keeps the Crown, at the corner of Old Cavendish-street - these three pots are his property - they are worth about 3s. 6d. - I missed them the same afternoon that they were taken.
Kelly's Defence. I did it because I was in want.
KELLY - GUILTY. Aged 22.
MILLS - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-185]
499. JOHN PRICE was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, one sheet, value 3s., the goods of Richard Archer.
ELIZABETH ARCHER. I am the wife of Richard Archer- we live in Plough-alley - on the 5th of February, the prisoner came and asked for a lodging - he went up to bed, and when he came down the next morning, he said he would go and be shaved - we then missed the sheet - I went out, and saw the prisoner coming back towards the house - he asked for a little bag which he had left behind him, containing thirty-six duplicates - I then said, "You have taken my sheet" - he said, "I have not;" but I found it round him.
Prisoner. I acknowledge I did take it.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
500. JOHN PRICE was again indicted for stealing, on the 4th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., and 1 jacket, value 2s., the goods of John Wilson.
JOHN WILSON. I am a sailor - I was lodging in Castle-street, Limehouse - on the 4th of February, the prisoner opened my door, and asked where he could get water to wash himself - I said, "Down stairs" - he went out into the next room, and the next morning I missed my jacket, handkerchief, and trousers.
JOHN ADAMS. I am an officer of the Thames-police - the prosecutor came to the office, and claimed the jacket which was on the prisoner's back.(The prisoner read a long statement of various hardships which he had endured at sea, and stating that the jacket was his own property.)
JOHN WILSON. I said there was a piece of tape to it, partly torn, which there is; and, on the starboard side, there were four buttons uncovered, and so there is - at the time he took my clothes, I had only 3d. in the world to get me a mouthful of victuals, and I grudged him that more than anything else.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-186]
501. JAMES SHEPLEY and SARAH HUME were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, 2 pewter pots, value 18d., the goods of Thomas Harwood.
THOMAS HARWOOD, JUN. My father keeps the Sieve public-house, in Church-street, Minories - I had seen Shipley there a day or two previous to his being taken - I went out and left him in the house - these two pots are my father's.
HENRY WEEKS (police-constable, K 216). I took these pots from the prisoner Hume, one was in each pocket, under her gown - I found her going to her own apartments, in Flower and Deane-street - I asked her what she had got - she said, "Nothing" - I felt the pots, and she begged me not to expose her in the street.
Hume. I opened my apron, and told him what I had.
Witness. No; you did not.
WILLIAM RENNAN. On the 29th of January, I was at the Castle public-house, Goodman's-stile - I saw Shepley there - he took a quart-pot, and put it into his hat - I told the publican of it - Shepley stopped about five minutes longer - he then went away - I followed him with Mr. Crutch, the publican - we stopped him at the corner of Chichester-street - Mr. Crutch took his hat off, and found the pot in it - he was liberated - on the 6th of February, I was going down Crutched-friars, and saw Hume standing at the corner of Savage-gardens - I knew her by her coming to the office to give Shepley a character - I then went into the Cheshire Cheese, and saw Shepley sitting with four or five pots round him - I came out - he came out, and crossed over to Hume, they went on, and stopped at a corner were he gave her something.
Shepley's Defence. I am an engineer but have been out of work eighteen months; this woman is my wife.
Hume's Defence. I was not at the Cheshire Cheese at all that morning - I am the wife of this prisoner, but Hume was my maiden name - I wished my husband to state that, because I wish to take my share of the trouble.
SHEPLEY - GUILTY. Aged 30.
HUME - GUILTY. Aged 23.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-187]
502. WILLIAM RICHARDSON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 1 pair of trousers, value 12s., the goods of John Oldchurch Webb.
JOHN WREN. I am a shopman to Mr. John Oldchurch Webb - he lives in the Strand - on the 17th of February, about six o'clock in the evening, the trousers were hanging at the door - the prisoner was seen to be looking at them, and he ran off with them - Carlisle and I pursued him - he was stopped near Covent-garden - in coming back we found the trousers in Bedford-street, in the way he had run.
RICHARD CARLISLE. I saw the prisoner take the trousers and run away with them - we pursued and took him.
WILLIAM POULTON. I saw the prisoner drop these trousers.
Prisoner's Defence. There was another young man standing at the door, he ran away at the same time.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Confined Six Weeks.

503. ELIZA WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 1 shawl, value 3s., the goods of James Gudge.
REBECCA GUDGE. I am the wife of James Gudge - we keep a potatoe shop, No. 4, White Horse-place, Mile End Old Town - my shawl was hanging over the potatoe bin on the 15th of February, the prisoner came in to offer a picture for sale - I refused to buy it - she went away - in about half an hour I went to take my milk pails, and missed my shawl - this is it.
JAMES RICHMAN FOLKARD. I am a pawnbroker. I tookin this shawl on the 15th of February, from the prisoner - I knew her before.

503. HENRY WHITE was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 1 coat, value 10s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 5s.; 1 waistcoat, value 3s.; and 2 handkerchiefs, value 2s.; of Thomas Awdos Maling, being in a vessel in the port of London, the same being a port of entry and discharge.
THOMAS AWDOS MALING. I belong to a brig from Sunderland - she was lying in Gun Dock, Wapping - on the 19th of February I first missed a coat, and then the other things which had been taken from the cabin - the prisoner was taken on board, but the property was found at the pawnbroker's - he has been my apprentice since June, 1832.
EUGENE KEARDON. I am a pawnbroker. I produce the coat, trousers, waistcoat, and handkerchief, which were pawned by the prisoner in the name of John Howard, on the 18th of February.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Confined Three Months.

504. MARTHA BRICKLE was indicted for stealing, on the 6th February, 1 coat, value 1l.; 1 pair of pattens, value 6d.; 1 gown, value 6d.; and one pair of boots, value 1s. 6d.; the goods of George Brickle.
GEORGE BRICKLE. I am cutter to a tailor, and live in Chapel-street, Curtain-road - the prisoner is my daughter, and was living at home with me - on the 6th of February she stole a coat, a pair of pattens, a gown, and a pair of boots - my wife got up in the morning and missed them.
ANN BRICKLE. I am the prosecutor's wife - the prisoner stole these things out of the room where they were kept - I work at the tailoring business - she is my own daughter, and is seventeen years old - I have one more child.
JOHN BOARD. I am a pawnbroker, and live at No. 42, Shoreditch - I have a coat and pair of boots pawned by the prisoner on the 6th of February; but, as I stated at the Police-office, I thought she had been sent with them by her parents, that they might come and get them for nothing - some time ago this man was living with another wife, who, I believe, was his lawful wife, and he in a very bad way obtained a jacket of me; but I gave it up rather than have any more trouble about it; I never heard any more of him till the morning the prisoner pawned these goods; and soon after she had pawned them, I saw him come and stand opposite my shop; he went away and came and stood there again for some time, and the next morning I heard that these things had been stolen - I said I was sorry I had taken them in, but I would not give them up, for I had given away pounds worth of things sooner than come here - when the prosecutor was at the office, he stated that he had not seen the girl all day, but I feel certain from his coming before my door that he had seen her just after she pawned them.
JAMES M'MICHEN (police-constable H 131). I took the prisoner on the evening of the 6th of February; I found on her two duplicates which led to these things.
JOHN BOARD. These are the duplicates - she pawned the coat for her father, but the boots in the name of Ann Harding.
GEORGE BRICKLE re-examined. The jacket that he states I got from him was one he took in unmade - I know not why he states what he has, unless it is because he was forced to give up that jacket - when I got up in the morning, I went out to look for my daughter, and I did this in order that she might be taken from her bad companions - I married my present wife in 1810, there was another woman who passed as my wife.
COURT. Q. Where is that wife? A. I don't know.
Q. In the course of your life how many wives have you had? A. This one and the other I lived with.
Q. Do you mean that you have not had two wives at once? A. Yes, I have; and they are both alive - I was tried here for bigamy, and fined 1s. - I allow my other wife 1s. 6d. a week for the child.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-191]
505. JANE BAXTER was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 2 silver forks, value 1l., the goods of William Abbott.
CHARLES BURFORD. I am in the service of William Abbott - he is a gentleman, and lives in Wynyat-place - the prisoner used to come to visit the cook - she was there on the 8th of February - we missed some property; I suspected her - we inquired, and some property was found at the pawnbroker's - she has been in the habit of coming there for three months.
JOHN MATTHEWS. I am a pawnbroker - I have two silver forks, pawned by the prisoner on the 10th of February - I have no doubt of her person.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing about them further than washing them up and leaving them in the kitchen.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Seven Years.

506. CHARLES COSTER and JOHN COSTER were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 10lbs. of copper bolts, value 10s.; and 20lbs. of copper rivets, value 7s., the goods of Edmund Pontifex and others.
CHARLES SCOTCHMER (police-constable G 125). On the 15th of February, at a quarter before eight o'clock at night, I was in Playhouse-yard - I saw the two prisoners pass me - Charles had a bundle under his arm - I suspected and followed them to a house in Playhouse-yard - I asked what they had in the bundle - they said, "Nothing," and they had no bundle - I searched John, and found under his coat these bolts now produced - I called another constable, and took them to the station-house - the bundle contained the ten pounds of copper rivets - they said two men on Clerkenwell-green gave them to them to sell.
RICHARD JENNINGS. Charles Coster worked with me - he was my hammer-man, in the employ of Mr. Pontifex - these rivets are some that he and I were employed in making that week - I know nothing of John - the name of the firm is Edmund Pontifex, Son, and Wood.
JOHN BROWNING (police-constable G 174). I took Charles Coster - these copper bolts were found on John - this bundle contains the other property.
SAMUEL LANE. I am in the employ of Messrs. Pontifex - Charles was employed there, but not John.( Thomas Powell and John Pilcher gave Charles Coster a good character.)
CHARLES COSTER - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Confined Three Months.
JOHN COSTER - NOT GUILTY.

507. THOMAS CAMPLIN was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 5 shillings, 4 sixpences, 24 halfpence, and 4 farthings, the monies of Henry Ranson, from his person.
HENRY RANSON. I sell fish in Shoreditch - on the 13th of February I had the misfortune to sprain my ancle in going across the road in Shoreditch, just before twelve o'clock at night; my wife was with me, and the prisoner was coming by - she said, "For God's sake assist my husband out of the road, as I think he has broken his ancle" - he gave his assistance, and when we got to Shoreditch church another took hold of my left arm - they led me on to Middlesex chapel; the other one then left me and ran away - I then sent my wife on to open the door, as I was in such pain - when I got between three and four doors of my house, the prisoner went away the same road as the other - I then put my hand into my pocket and missed my money, about 9s. in all; about 2s. in copper - there was one farthing I can swear to - I called "Stop thief" - I saw the prisoner stopped at the corner - I have seen the farthing since.
DAVID JONES. I was near the end of Thomas's-street - I heard the cry of "Stop thief," and saw the prisoner running towards Shoreditch church as fast as he could - I pursued him - he gained upon me - a policeman crossed towards him - he turned back, and I took him - I gave him to the parties who cried "Stop thief" - the prosecutor's wife said that he had robbed them of all the money they were about to pay their rent with - there was some silver found on him; 5s. in silver, and 1s. 71/4d. in copper - he objected to being searched, and denied having any money about him.
JEREMIAH O'SULLIVAN (police-constable, N 220.) I took the prisoner - what has been stated is correct - I found the money stated on him - this is the farthing which the prosecutor can swear to - I took the coppers and 3s. 6d. in silver out of his different pockets.
Prisoner. The magistrate asked him if he could swear to any of the money, and he said "No."
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-194]
508. JACOB FRIEDLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 1 shirt, value 3s; 1 waistcoat, value 1s. 6d.; 1 pair of stockings, value 1s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 1s. 6d.; 1 tobacco box, value 1s.; and 1 penknife, value 6d., the goods of William Gray.
WILLIAM GRAY. I lost these things from Mr. Griffiths', No. 13, Noble-street, St. Luke's - the prisoner lodged with me - I lost them on the 26th of November, and he left the house directly - on Thursday last my master brought him home, and he asked me what one thing and another was worth - I said they were worth 17s.
THOMAS GRIFFITHS. I employed these two men - they both lodged in one room - on the 26th of November the prisoner came down with a bundle - we had given him warning to leave - he washed himself and went away with the bundle without his breakfast - he left some cards of steel pens behind him - when I took him he asked the prosecutor what the articles were worth.
FRANCIS GRANEY (police-constable G 87). I took the prisoner - he said he would give 8s. to settle it, or if not, he did not care whether he got three months or seven years of it.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor wanted to settle with me; he asked me 1l., then 15s., and then 12s., and I offered him 8s.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Confined Three Months.
