510. ELIZABETH KEYMER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 2 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 2 sixpences, the monies of Thomas Keen, from his person.
THOMAS KEEN. I deal in yeast - I fell in with the prisoner on 12th of February, about seven o'clock in the evening - I had seen her once or twice before - I was a little fresh - she asked me to give her something to drink - she took me to a private house - I sent for two pots of beer- there were four or five of us - we then went to a gin shop and had half a pint of gin - another lady then came up, and she asked me to give them some more gin, which I did- she then said if we would go over the way, she would stand a pot, and we did - the prisoner then asked me to give them a little more; I said I could not; the prisoner then took my jacket off my arm, and took two half-crowns, two sixpences, and a shilling out of my pocket - she ran out, and I pursued, but lost her - I saw her at the station-house the next day.
JOHN CLARK. I saw the prosecutor come into the public-house with a pipe in his mouth - the prisoner took something from his pocket, but I do not think it was without his own will.
THOMAS COOPER. I am an officer - I took the prisoner the next day - she said he gave her the money to stop with her.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-197]
510. ABRAHAM SIMMONS was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM BRADBURY. I am a hay salesman - the prisoner was in my service nearly twelve months - it was his duty to receive money, and to account to me for it - when he sold any hay, he should bring the cart into the market, and give me the money - this hay was my own - he came into the market on the 1st of February, about a quarter past two, with his team, and then he disappeared - I waited some time, he did not come; I went to some places, and could not find him - on the Wednesday afterwards I saw him in custody - he had given me no notice of leaving me.
JOSEPH MAZE. I paid the prisoner 4l. for a load of hay.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury. - Confined Three Weeks.
[trialAccount t18340220-198]
511. ABRAHAM ABERCROMBY was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 ring, value 11s., the goods of James Sodo, from his person.
JAMES SODO. I am a hat-manufacturer, and live in Bethnal-green - on the 14th of February, about ten minutes past eleven at night, this happened - we had had part of two glasses of gin and water among six of us - I was coming across Hare-street Fields, and saw the prisoner talking to two females - he said, "How do you do, Sir?" - I said, "How do you do?" and put out my hand to shake hands with him- I had a ring on my finger, and he slipped it off - I told him not to take it off - he said he had got no ring, but he handed it to a female - I seized him, and handed him to the officer.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did not you take him to be a friend? A. I thought at first he was one of my customers - I could not keep my ring on, for he is stronger than I am.
JOSHUA HARCOURT (police-constable H 51). I was called, and the prosecutor had the prisoner by the collar - he said the prisoner had taken a ring from his finger - there were two women near him - the prosecutor had been drinking, but knew what he was about.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the prosecutor collar the prisoner? A. No; he said afterwards, that the prisoner gave the ring to one of the women; it has not been found.
ELEANOR FLACK. On the night of this robbery, I met the prosecutor at Three Coldt-corner - he greatly insulted me, and followed to a house where I was going - I asked him what he meant, and he put his hands in my face- I told him he took great liberties - I could not get into the house, where I was going to sleep, and the prisoner came up, and the prosecutor gave charge of him - he had no ring on when he put his hands in my face.
COURT. Q. Was any body with you? A. No - I am not married - I am in the workhouse - the prosecutor was very tipsy. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-199]
OLD COURT. - Wednesday, February 26th.
Second London Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
512. BENJAMIN WINCH was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of January, 1 saddle-tree, value 3s. 6d., the goods of John Asbury.
JOHN NORMAN. I am a sadler, and live in Milton-street - on the 18th of January, the prisoner brought me a saddle-tree for sale, and asked 1s. 6d. for it - I asked him how he came by it, as I knew he was Mr. Reynolds's, the undertaker's servant - he said he had lived with his master a long time - I bought the saddle of him, and afterwards sent for his master, and found out the owner.
JOSEPH ASBURY. This is my saddle-tree - it was taken out of my shop, in White-street, Little Moorfields - I am a sadler - I knew the prisoner, as his master is a customer of mine - on the 18th of January, he came to my shop with some harness to be repaired, and went away; and about an hour and a half after he was gone, a person came and asked if I had lost anything, and I found the saddle-tree at Norman's.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When did you see it last? A. The day it was stolen - my shop-window was not open - it did not hang outside the shop.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18340220-200]
513. ELIZA DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 3s.; the goods of William George Robinson.
HENRY GRIFFIN. I am a linen and woollen draper - on the morning of Wednesday, the 19th of February, I was on Ludgate-hill, returning from the theatre, about two o'clock in the morning - the gas lamps were burning - it was a very light night - I was walking with the prosecutor, who had been to the theatre with me - as we returned home we saw the prisoner; she made up to us, and took hold of the prosecutor's arm - I pushed her away - she came a second time - I pushed her away again - she came a third time - I threatened to give her in charge - the prosecutor felt and missed his handkerchief, and charged her with it - she said he might search her - he felt her bosom, and as she turned round I saw the handkerchief drop from her - he recognised it as his property - it dropped from her right hand.
WILLIAM GEORGE ROBINSON. I am a farmer - I was with Griffin - the prisoner accosted us two or three times and took hold of my arm - I had my handkerchief in my left hand pocket - I felt her take it out - I turned round and charged her with it - she denied it, and said something about searching her - I saw her drop it, at least I saw it before it got on the ground, close to her.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. It is false - the gentleman put his hand into his pocket on my leaving him, and his friend said,"Have you all your property safe?" - he said, "I do not know" - he looked down, and his friend said, "You have got his handkerchief" - I said "Search me," and then he said, "Oh there it is" - his friend had three or four handkerchiefs in his pocket, and in pulling them out I suppose he must have dropped it.
WILLIAM GEORGE ROBINSON re-examined. I had two or three handkerchiefs in my pocket, but very different ones to this; they were not all in the same pocket - there was nobody near us - I had had a glass or two of liquor, but knew what I was about.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-201]
514. WILLIAM FIGGINS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of William Bearre Edwards, from his person.
WILLIAM BEARRE EDWARDS. I live in Aldersgate-street - I have been clerk to a wholesale house in Cheapside - I was passing out of Goswell-street into Aldersgate-street, about half-past eight or nine o'clock, on Sunday evening, the 2nd of February - I had a pocket handkerchief in my coat pocket - having occasion to use it, I put my hand in my pocket - at that moment a young woman nudged my elbow, and said, "Look behind you" - I hadbefore that observed a young man following me, which I did not take notice of, except feeling my pocket, and the handkerchief was then safe; but when I had occasion to use it, the young woman told me to look behind me - I then saw the young man walk away from me and join two more, one of whom was the prisoner - they all turned down Aldersgate-street - I told the watchman I suspected them, and he followed them - I do not think the prisoner took it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-202]
516. ELEANOR KENNEDY was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of January, 3 ounces of fur, value 5s.; the goods of Isaac Lialter, her master.
ISAAC LIALTER. I am a furrier, and live in Middle-street, Aldgate - the prisoner was in my employ - I gave her so many skins to manufacture on the premises, which she was to bring me at night - on the 27th I gave her (about the middle of the day) thirty hare skins, to cut the fur from the pelt, and bring down the produce of the skins, which is the fur - when she came down at night, I weighed the work, and found three or four ounces deficient, having weighed the skins in the morning - I said, "Is this all the produce of the day's work?" - she said, "Yes, it is" - she went into the other shop - I said, "You must come back, I shall send for the street-keeper - I suspect you have got some of my fur about you" - she hesitated a good deal, and took off her bonnet - I said, "What have you in your bonnet?" - she cried, and said it was the first time she ever took any, and hoped I would forgive her; and in the bonnet were three ounces of the same fur as she had to work on.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 59. - Recommended to mercy. - Confined for Three Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-203]
517. WILLIAM BALLARD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d., the goods of William Hartnell, from his person.
WILLIAM HARTNELL. On the 20th of February, about five o'clock in the afternoon, I was walking down Whitechapel - I felt something going out of my pocket - I turned round suddenly, and saw the prisoner drop my handkerchief from his hand behind him - I had laid hold of him at that time - I took it up and gave him to the officer - I was talking with Crumpton, the officer, at the same time.
CHARLES CRUMPTON. I am an officer - I was walking with Hartnell - I saw him turn his head round - I turned, and saw the handkerchief in the prisoner's hand - he dropped it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY.* Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-204]
518. GEORGE KING was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of January, 1 handkerchief, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Ephraim Jacobs, from his person.
HENRY GOODMAN. I was walking in Union-street, between twelve and one o'clock, on the 27th of January, and saw two young men behind a gentleman, and suspected them - I kept my eye on one of them for about two minutes - they were walking fast together, behind the prosecutor - I then saw a handkerchief drawn from the prosecutor's pocket, by the other, and thrown to the prisoner - they were both close to the prosecutor - I could not see which hand took it - but I think it was the other- I saw the other give it to the prisoner, who pushed it into his bosom, and both ran off down a street on the other side - I asked the gentleman if he had lost anything - he said yes, his handkerchief - I ran after the prisoner, the other ran another way, and at the bottom of the street, he saw me coming, and threw it over his left shoulder - I caught hold of him, brought him back, and took it up - I brought him to the prosecutor.
EPHRAIM JACOBS. I was walking in Union-street - Goodman asked if I had lost anything - I felt my pocket, and missed my handkerchief - I crossed over the road, and saw the prisoner throw the handkerchief over his shoulder, and, immediately after that, the witness took him - this is my handkerchief.
GUILTY.* Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-205]
519. ABRAHAM JACOBS and JOHN BRYAN were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of a man whose name is unknown, from his person.
CHARLES CHAMBERS. I am one of the City police; on the 19th of February, at a quarter after six o'clock in the evening, I was at the corner of Arundel-street, Aldgate - I saw the prisoners in company together, and watched them for about five minutes - I saw Jacobs put his right hand into a gentleman's coat pocket, and pull a handkerchief out, and give it to Bryan - they were both within half a yard of each other - Bryan put it into his breast, and I laid hold of him - he directly dropped it at his feet - I stooped to pick it up, and he tried to get away - Jacobs ran away - I took him about twenty minutes afterwards in Gracechurch-street, as I was taking Bryan to the watchhouse - I have seen them both for about a dozen times a-week for the last eighteen months, and knew them well - I called out to the gentleman several times, but could not make him hear - I don't know his name.
Jacob's Defence. I was going up Gracechurch-street, the policeman tapped me on the shoulder, and said, "I want you, my lad, you must come to the watchhouse with me;" and when I got there he said, "I take you for picking a gentleman's pocket, and giving it to the other prisoner;" whom I had never seen before in my life.
JACOBS - GUILTY.* Aged 19.
BRYAN - GUILTY.* Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-206]
Before Mr. Recorder.
520. MATTHEW RYANT was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Benjamin Lewis, from his person.
RICHARD SPARKS. I am patrol of St. Dunstan's in the West. On the evening of the 10th of February, I saw the prisoner in Fleet-street - I saw him take a handkerchief out of a gentleman's pocket, who was walking with his brother up Fleet-street - I took him into custody - he dropped the handkerchief - I picked it up - called the gentlemen back, and one of them said, "He has got my handkerchief" - we went to the station-house, and the gentleman gavecharge of him - I produce the handkerchief which I saw him take, and afterwards drop.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What time was it? A. A quarter before nine in the evening - I am quite sure I saw him take it - I was close, following him with my toes to his heels - I know him perfectly well before, by having followed him in the street - when I first saw him I was on the opposite side of the way, and I directly ran across and followed him - the handkerchief was not out of the pocket when I crossed over - several persons came up and said,"Let him go;" and he screamed and brought several people, and caught hold of a post - there might be other persons passing at the time.
WILLIAM LEWIS. I am the brother of Benjamin Lewis- I was walking with him in Fleet-street - he is too ill to attend here - our attention was called by Sparks - my brother returned with me - he immediately saw his handkerchief in the witness's hands, and claimed it as his property - I did not lose mine - the prisoner was near enough to hear him claim it - he attended before the magistrate next morning - he lives with me, and I know he had such a handkerchief as that.(John Blankly, hair-dresser, No. 5, Ritchbell-place, Lambs-conduit-street; and John Jones, hair-dresser, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY.* Aged 15. - Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-207]
518. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of a man whose name is unknown, from his person.
JAMES WALTER BREWER. I am a ward-officer - on the afternoon of the 19th of February, I saw the prisoner in Star-alley, Fenchurch-street, about half-past four o'clock - he was in company with two others - I saw one of his companions take a pocket-handkerchief out of a gentleman's pocket, and pass it to the prisoner, who put it into his pocket- I immediately seized the prisoner, and called after the gentlemen - there were two walking together - they did not hear me - I do not know either of their names - I produce the handkerchief.
Cross-examined by MR. STAMMERS. Q. In what part of the street was it? A. Star-alley - I took the handkerchief out of the prisoner's pocket - I was within a yard of him - the prisoner and his companions were walking together - one of them had his arm on the prisoner's shoulder - I cannot say which it was, they walked so close together.(George Coad, shoemaker, No. 28, Phoenix-street, Somers Town, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18340220-208]
519. JOHN SPATS was indicted for a misdemeanour.
Mr. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES COLLIS. I am a shopman to Mr. Lynn, who is a shell-fish-monger in Fleet-street - I know the defendant - I knew him in Mr. Lovegrove's employ, ever since Mr. Lovegrove has had the London Coffee-house, which is about two years - I was not aware that he had left - during those two years he was in the habit of coming to our shop from Mr. Lovegrove, on Mr. Lovegrove's account; and I furnished him with anything he came for on Mr. Lovegrove's account, which Mr. Lovegrove always paid for - on the 10th of January the prisoner came and said, "I want four slices of brawn and a pot of shrimps" - that was such an order as he was in the habit of giving from Mr. Lovegrove when in his service - he was dressed in a hairy cap, and an old blue jacket - he had no apron - we had furnished the same sort of thing before for Mr. Lovegrove - he then said, "I am to take half a dozen herrings, if you are not open to-morrow" - I asked him who said so; and his answer was, I believe, "My governor" - I said, "Your governor has made a mistake: to-morrow is Saturday instead of Sunday" - I delivered him the brawn - we had no shrimps - I had just sold the last pot, and did not deliver him the herrings - the value of the brawn, at the price sold to Mr. Lovegrove, is 2s. 8d. - it was one pound and a quarter.
Q. Did you deliver it to the prisoner, supposing from what he said that he was the servant of Mr. Lovegrove? A. I did: I did not know to the contrary - Mr. Lynn always books the goods himself - it was booked to Mr. Lovegrove - I supposed he came from Mr. Lovegrove - I understood by the word "governor," that he meant Mr. Lovegrove - on Monday, the 13th of January, he came again, and said,"I want three slices of brawn" - he said that to Mr. James Lynn, and I gave them to him by Mr. Lynn's direction.
JAMES LYNN, JUN. I am the son of James Lynn, who is a shell-fish-monger, in Fleet-street - I knew the defendant in Mr. Lovegrove's employ, previous to the 13th of January, for about two years - he came to my father's shop during those two years frequently for brawn, herrings, potted shrimps, and lobsters, for Samuel Lovegrove, his master, who keeps the London Coffee House and Tavern, Ludgate-hill - he has said, on former occasions, that he came from Mr. Lovegrove's, but not always - while he was in Mr. Lovegrove's service, he used to come for these articles on his account, and received them for him, and carried them away himself. - he received them from our shopman and servants on different occasions - they were sold and delivered to him by my father, myself, and the servants - my father has no partner - on the 10th of January, I was not present, but in consequence of a communication made to me by Collis, four slices of brawn were booked to Mr. Lovegrove - on the morning of the 13th of January the defendant came and said to me, "I want three slices of brawn" - I said, "Collis, cut that brawn" - he did so - I said to the defendant, "How many sons has your master in the business?" - he said, "Two," - I said, "Both in the business?" - he said, "Yes; both of them" - I did not mention Mr. Lovegrove's name - I went down the street, and fetched a policeman - I told him to wait outside, and he was taken into custody by the policeman - I gave him the goods, let him go out, and put him in the policeman's hands.
COURT. Q. Then you did not believe the pretence he made? A. No; not on that day, because I was aware of it on the Saturday night - he did not obtain these goods in consequence of the pretence - I delivered them to complete the case against him - Mr. Lovegrove paid for the goods as long as the prisoner was in his service.
MR. SAMUEL LOVEGROVE. I am proprietor of the London Coffee House - the defendant was in my employ ever since I became proprietor of the London Coffee House,for two years nearly - I frequently sent him to Mr. James Lynn's shop for brawn, lobsters, herrings, shrimps, and such things - the accounts were sent in to me generally every week, by Mr. Lynn, and paid by my authority - I discharged the defendant (I cannot exactly say the date, but I think) the latter end of September, or early in October - I am certain he was not in my service at the end of October, and after that time he had no authority from me to obtain anything from Mr. Lynn on my account - I did not employ him at all since October - he was an errand-boy or porter - my house is in the parish of St. Martin, Ludgate.
GUILTY of obtaining the goods on the 10th of January. -
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18340220-209]
NEW COURT, Wednesday, February 26th, 1834.
Fifth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
JOHN FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1 purse, value 1d.; 1 sovereign, and 3 sixpences, the property of Charles Joseph Egleton, from his person.
DANIEL CLARKE. I was at a sale shop in Piccadilly about three o'clock in the afternoon, on the 13th of February; the prosecutor was standing before me, and I was looking over his catalogue - the prisoner pressed between us, and pressed against the prosecutor - I looked round and saw another person pressing more than there was any occasion for - I drew back, and saw the prisoner draw his hand away from the prosecutor's right side, and he handed the contents of his hand to a person on his left - he appeared to have something in his hand - the person walked away immediately out of the shop, and the prisoner followed him - I told the prosecutor, and the prisoner was followed.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was not the room very much crowded? A. It was - the prisoner was close to me - I saw his arm - I did not see his hand - I saw him take his hand from the prosecutor's pocket.
Q. Did he take his hand from his pocket? A. He took it from his side - he is one of the swell mob, and has been so for years - I have had threats held out to me.
Q. Did you not tell my Lord in the first instance that you saw him take his hand from his side, and now you have sworn it was from the pocket? A. From towards his pocket.
Q. Which of the three is truth? was it from his side, from his pocket, or from towards his pocket? A. It is all the same thing, he drew his arm from his pocket - I am a paper hanger - I went to the sale on purpose to buy- the prisoner pressed against me - there was no one between me and him - there were twenty-five or thirty people in the shop.
COURT. Q. Are you sure that the person who pressed against you was the prisoner? A. Yes; his right arm pressed against me, and he pressed against the prosecutor, and when he was taking something from him I felt his elbow rub against my side, and I saw him pass the contents of his hand to a person; he appeared to have something in his hand, but I did not see any thing in his hand - I saw the motion of his arm.
Q. What do you mean by saying he appeared to pass the contents of his hand to his neighbour? A. I saw him draw his arm up from the prosecutor's right side, and pass it over to another person, but there was something in his hand, or the other would not have left immediately.
CHARLES JOSEPH EGLETON. I had a purse in my pocket with two sovereigns and three sixpences in it at the sale shop - I took one sovereign out of my purse to pay in a deposit, in case I could not get at my purse when the place was crowded - I put my purse into my pocket again with one sovereign and three sixpences in it - when Mr. Clarke spoke to me I missed my purse, and went out and took the prisoner - I had not seen him in the shop.
THOMAS CHAPMAN (police-constable C 69). I was on duty at the watchhouse in Little Wild-street. I received information and went to Derby-court, where I saw the prosecutor with the prisoner - he gave him in charge for robbing him - the prisoner said, "You may search me, I have not got his purse."
MR. EGLETON. When I went and collared the prisoner, I told him he had robbed me of my purse.
Witness for the Defence.
JOHN AVANN. I am a builder, and live at No. 1, Meredith-street, Clerkenwell - I have known the prisoner five years - he lodged with me a year and a half ago - he certainly is not one of the swell mob to my knowledge - on the day of the sale I met him in Piccadilly, and went with him to the sale room - I went to buy some articles, but I found the lots I wanted had been sold - I think the prisoner and I stayed in the room about twenty minutes - I came out with him - he certainly did not hand me a purse or anything else - he has been a respectable man.
COURT. Q. Do you swear he did not hand any one else anything? A. That I cannot tell, the room was very crowded.( John Russell, watchmaker and working jeweller, William-street, Clerkenwell; John Bicknell, commercial agent, No. 8, Durham-street, Vauxhall; and Thomas Kimber, of No. 275, Strand, gave the prisoner a good character.)
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-210]
521. RICHARD DEANE was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of February, seven yards of velvet, value 2l. 12s., the goods of Thomas Chester, and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM CHESTER. I am eleven years old - I know the nature of an oath - I am the brother of Thomas Chester - he lives at No. 1, James-street, Bethnal-green - he went out about half-past eight o'clock on the morning of the 4th of February - I was left at home - I went out after my brother was gone out, to the end of the window, to see a little boy put some wood into a truck - I am almost certain I left the room door latched when I went into the street; any one could get into the room by lifting up the latch - the little boy who was there told me something, and I saw the prisoner running from our house - I knew him, and called to him, " Dick Deane " - I think he must have heard me, but he did not answer.
JURY. Q. What did you call after him for? A. Because he was running from my brother's house, and had something under his arm under his coat - I went into the house, and saw my brother's work cut off the roll - I had seen that roll before I went into the street, and it was allright - I was not in the street above five minutes - I did not see anybody else about.
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. Does your brother keep pigeons? A. Yes, in the same room - the window is not kept open - there was nobody at home but me- I had been there from half-past eight o'clock - my brother was there then - and he went out - I did not see the prisoner's face - he did not turn round to me - I did not see him come out of the room.
JOHN COOPER. I was with Chester that morning, and saw the prisoner come out at the door - he turned and looked both ways - I said to Chester, "Do you know him?" and he called after him several times - he appeared to have a bundle under his coat - he was running.
Cross-examined. Q. How near were you to your friend when the prisoner came out? A. He was on the side of me - the prisoner looked into my face - I am not certain whether the prisoner's coat was buttoned, but there was something sticking out at the back of his coat - it was not like a coat, it was a flat bundle - I cannot tell whether it was in paper, as I could not see through the coat.
MARY GREENLEAF. I live opposite the prosecutor - I was sweeping my door about nine o'clock that day - I saw the prisoner come out with a bundle, and the boy said,"Dick Deane, what have you got there?"
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the bundle distinctly? A. Yes - I saw it - I stated at the office that he had the bundle under his arm; I cannot tell whether it was under his coat, nor what size or colour it was - I was on the other side of the way - I believe there are two lodgers in Chester's house.
MARGARET ANN BEST. I am the wife of William Best - I was in Church-street, Spitalfields, that morning, about half-past nine o'clock - I met the prisoner going out of Brick-lane, with some velvet under his arm inside his coat- I said "Halloo," and he said, "Flare up, and you shall have a velvet bonnet" - I said nothing to him, and he ran off.
Cross-examined. Q. Could you see the velvet? A. Yes; the front of his coat was open; it was black velvet with white edges.
THOMAS CHESTER. I lost seven yards of velvet that morning, which was worth 2l. 12s. 6d.
THOMAS GRANT (police-constable H 76). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office - I know the prisoner is the man (read).
Witness for the Prisoner.
JOSEPH SMITH. I have known the prisoner two years - on the morning of the 4th of February, about nine o'clock, the prisoner's wife came to my house, and asked me to let him come and finish some work, which I said he should - I put on my hat and went to his house - he was washing himself - he left his house with me, and went to the door of my house; but he did not go in, as he had borrowed a coat of a friend, which he had tied up in a blue and white handkerchief under his arm - I did not see him again till he was in custody.
HENRY PALMER. I lent the prisoner a coat, which he brought and returned to me on the 4th of February, about a quarter past nine o'clock; and he stayed in my shop a quarter of an hour, or twenty minutes.
Prisoner. The last time I was here, it was a warning to me; and I have got my living ever since by hard work.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Confined for One Year.
[trialAccount t18340220-211]
522. CATHERINE HICKEY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 1 pair of breeches, value 1s. 6d.; 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d.; 10 shillings, 2 sixpences, and 3 halfpence, the goods and monies of Timothy Flynn.
TIMOTHY FLYNN. I am a labourer, and live in Cock-alley - the prisoner lives with my son; but she tells me she is not his wife - on the morning of the 5th of February I was in bed - about two o'clock the policeman came into my house and inquired for my son, Patrick Flynn; but he was not there - the prisoner came into the passage; but she did not come into the room - when the policeman was gone, I went to bed again; but I cannot say whether I locked the door, I was in such a flurry, being ill - about an hour afterwards I heard a footstep, and saw the prisoner in my room - she went over to where my breeches were, and took them, and a silk handkerchief, ten shillings, two sixpences, and three halfpence, which were in my breeches pocket - I got out of bed as well I could, and called, "Stop thief" - the prisoner got out of the room, and I went after her; but I could not take her - I have not seen any of them since; I went to the station the next day and stated what I had lost - when I ran after her, I had nothing on but my shirt - I saw her by the gas-light.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was there any gaslight in your room? A. No; but one is opposite my window - my shutters were not shut - I did not hear her open the door - I heard her foot - I did not say anything to her till she was going out - I then called, "Stop thief" - she did not give me time to speak to her when she was in the room - I get my living by labour - I travel the country; and if a gentleman asks me to get a bottle of gin or rum for him, I do so - I should not know whisky in this country from water - I have quarrelled with the prisoner - she never put me into the station - I have been there for getting drunk, but not for anything else - I have been at the Two Bells, Whitechapel - the prisoner did not give me into custody for beating her - my wife was in bed with me when the prisoner took my breeches - the officer had disturbed her when he came in before - I do not know whether she was awake when the prisoner came in - I found her awake when I went up again - the prisoner robbed me twice before - I charged her with the robbery at Lambeth-street - my wife took her there; but she forgave her - my son lived with me; but I turned him out, for his behaviour - he lived with the prisoner in Essex-street - when I called after the prisoner, my next door neighbour put her head out of her window and asked what brought me out so late in the morning - I told her that Carroty Kit had robbed me.
COURT. Q. You know you are on your oath to speak the truth - do you mean to swear that this prisoner came in and took your breeches? A. Yes.
ANN HINTON. I live next door to Flynn; about three o'clock that morning I heard the alarm of "Stop thief" - I got out of bed, opened the window, and saw Flynn and Hickey - she was going across the ruins as if from Flynn's house - he was standing at the door, and he said, "Carroty Kit has been and robbed me of my breeches, and my money and handkerchief" - he was in his shirt and night-cap - the prisoner had a bundle under her arm.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Flynn? A. Seven or eight months - I live next door to him - I do not remember the prisoner living there - Flynn's son has lived there - my husband is dead - it is about three months since I saw a man named Maquire - he is in Hertford gaol - he is no relation of mine - he has called at my house - he never passed as my husband - I never lived with him as his wife - he did say when he came out of the country he would marry me, if I wished it.
MR. PHILLIPS to TIMOTHY FLYNN. Q. Were you ever in custody for an assault? A. Not to my knowledge - this policeman came into my house, as he was told I had some liquor there - he and I fought and beat one another - I was taken to the House of Correction, and was there two months - but I forgot that.
MARMADUKE LOVELL (police-constable G 144). I went to see for Patrick Flynn, as the prisoner told me he had committed felony, but I could not find him - I disturbed the old man - he got up, and opened the door - I took the prisoner the next night.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you find the breeches? A. No: Flynn got up, and was a good while opening the door for me - I saw a woman there - she asked what I wanted of her son - they were both awake when I went down - Flynn went with me, and shut the door - the prisoner was there - she went out with me; and he said, "Ah, Carroty Kit, is it you?" I did not stop to hear the door shut.(The prisoner put in a written defence, protesting her innocence; and stating that she had induced the policeman to go to the prosecutor's house, suspecting his son who had deserted her was to be found there.)
JOHN CONNOLLY. I have known Flynn two years - I should be very sorry to believe him on his oath - he has perjured himself once or twice in what he has stated here.
THOMAS GRANT (police-constable H 76). I have known him more than three years - I would not give him credit - he swore when he saw the prisoner going up the street, that he would transport her if he could - I have heard him say that fifty times.
COURT to TIMOTHY FLYNN. Q. Did you say you would transport her if you could? A. Yes.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-212]
523. GEORGE ROWLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of February, 18 pair of shoes, value 2l. 15s., the goods of Walter Pitman, his master; and ELIZABETH CLARK for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
WILLIAM DAMAN (police-constable, G 161). I took the prisoner Clark, and found at her house thirteen pair of shoes - I showed them to Mr. Pitman, and while I was doing that Rowland came in - Mr. Pitman showed the shoes to him, and asked if he knew them - he said he did, and they were Mr. Pitman's property - I then asked Rowland if he recollected my speaking to him in Castle-street, about a fortnight before - he said he did not - I then asked him if he did not go to No. 5, Friendly-place - he hesitated some time, and then said he did - that was where Clark lives, and where I found these shoes - he said he went there to see Mrs. Townsend, which is the name Clark went by.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe it is a bad house? A. Yes.
GEORGE AVERY (police-constable, G 175). I went with Daman, and found these shoes - Rowland said he did not recollect seeing me in the street.
WALTER PITMAN. The prisoner Rowland was in my employ as a confidential shopman - these shoes were brought to my shop by the two officers - I looked over them, and identified them - when Rowland came in I called him round and said, "This is neither retail nor shoplifting, this is wholesale work; whose property is this?" - he said, "It is yours, Sir" - I traced some other shoes to the pawnbroker from duplicates we found in Clark's box - here are eighteen pair - they are all mine.
Cross-examined. Q. You keep several shops? A. Yes- I think I have some recollection of seeing Clark at my shop.
CHARLES WALTER. I am a pawnbroker - I have one pair of woman's shoes pawned in the name of Ann Pett - one pair in the name of Elizabeth Pett, and one pair in the name of Elizabeth Townsend, by the prisoner Clark.
JOHN PAWLEY. I am a pawnbroker - I have one pair of shoes pawned by Clark.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18340220-213]
524. GEORGE ROWLAND was again indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 pair of leather boot fronts, value 2s. 6d.; and eight ounces of leather, value 1s. 2d. the goods of Walter Pitman, his master.
GEORGE AVERY (police-constable). I found at Clark's a large leather travelling trunk, and some property in it - I took Rowland at No. 4, Denmark-court, Golden-lane - I found on him a key, which opened a tin box, but I only found some copper money in that - I found some soles and a pair of leather boot fronts, in a hat-box in his room - he directed me to the house, and said he lived in the front room, one pair - I there found a woman who answered to the name of Mrs. Rowland.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. The prisoner was not there? A. No; he was at the station .
WALTER PITMAN. This leather is mine, and is worth about 4s. - the other I have no mark on - the prisoner had no business to take any to his own house .
Cross-examined . Q. Is he a workman ? A. No; I can swear to these boot fronts - they are my own cutting ; and I can swear to this piece by a stain on it.
Prisoner's Defence . I bought that front and blocked it myself .( Elizabeth Avery, and Joseph James, gave the prisoner a good character.) (See page 255.)
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
